# RESEARCH benchmarks
## summary
Quantitative scaling data for Modelica compilation/simulation clusters around Casella's ScalableTestSuite (STS) and its derivatives. The canonical cross-strategy numbers come from Braun/Casella/Bachmann (Modelica 2017), who benchmarked nine STS models at three sizes each under four solution strategies in OpenModelica 1.12: default dense ODE mode with DASSL+tearing (OD), causalized sparse ODE with IDA/KLU/KINSOL (OS), full sparse DAE mode (DA), and a reduced "dynamic DAE" mode (DD). Sparse solvers helped in 7 of 9 benchmarks, with speedups from ~2x (SteamPipe, OneDHeatTransfer) to ~60x (TransmissionLineEquations_N_1280: 241.1 s OD vs 4.6-6.1 s sparse). DAE mode vs sparse ODE mode is model-dependent: multibody chains (StringModelica_N_64: 86.5 s OD vs 3.76 s DA) strongly favor DAE mode, while MSL-based TransmissionLineModelica is ~5x faster in sparse ODE mode than full DA (9.0 s vs 47.8 s) because of redundant algebraic variables that DA drags through every Newton iteration. Power grids are the flagship DAE-mode case: their DAE is sparse but the causalized ODE Jacobian is dense (all synchronous generators couple through the phasor network), so implicit ODE solvers are structurally disadvantaged; the Irish grid model RETE_C (56,386 eq.) simulated a 20 s transient in 0.96-3.18 s and the Italian high-fidelity grid RETE_G (593,886 eq.) in 60-123 s in DA mode. The 2021 OpenModelica/RTE ScalableTestGrids study found OMC code generation acceptable only up to ~200 grid components and simulation performance reasonable up to ~4,000 components, with scalar C code generation identified as the bottleneck requiring "vectorized code generation" breakthroughs.

For the OpenModelica frontend/backend rewrite: the new frontend (Pop et al., Modelica 2019) cut STS flattening times by 5-30x vs the old frontend (DistributionSystemLinear_N_112_M_112, 779,312 eq.: 32.3 s new vs 1076.5 s old, and 74.2 s in Dymola 2019) and 20-200x for MultiBody GUI annotation queries; with scalarization disabled (-d=-nfScalarize) the SolarSystem n=100,000 model flattens in 2.95 s vs 2450 s scalarized (and 14,459 s in Dymola). The new array-preserving ("pseudo-array") backend of Abdelhak/Bachmann shows, in their 2022 workshop slides on CascadedFirstOrder up to scaling factor 24,000, old-backend total times climbing to ~5,000 s while the new backend stays roughly two orders of magnitude lower in backend time (~12 s old vs ~1-2 s new; C compilation ~80 s old). As of the Feb 2026 workshop the new backend's array handling is still "work in progress" for algorithms/tearing. On the current OMC v1.24 STS report, 243/263 models simulate, but several (HarmonicOscillatorNetwork_N_320, DistributionSystemLinear_N_56_M_56) hit the 300 s build-time or 925 s total timeouts, and the STS README states the bottleneck "is almost invariably C compilation," with -O0 compilation 3-4x faster at a 20-30% simulation-speed penalty.

MARCO (Politecnico di Milano, Modelica 2023) is the strongest published data point for LLVM-based direct code generation: array-aware causalization plus MLIR/LLVM-IR emission (skipping C entirely) compiled a 14,776,202-equation methanol heat-exchanger network in 10.13 s and a 4,465,160-equation 3D thermal-chip model in 86.24 s on a ~1,500 EUR i9-12900KF machine, sizes "beyond the current capabilities of the OpenModelica compiler"; compile time is one or more orders of magnitude below run time (33,575 s and 605 s explicit-Euler simulations respectively). The underlying theory (Fioravanti et al., ACM TOMS 2023) proves optimal array-aware matching is NP-complete and gives a polynomial heuristic achieving effectively O(1) matching cost per array when models come from PDE discretizations. Earlier OMC experiments with array-preserving code generation (EOOLT 2019 benchmark-suite paper) reported 1-2 orders of magnitude build-time reduction and 2-3x smaller generated code. The 2025 Modiator work (Elmqvist/Otter, Modelica 2025 "Resizable Arrays") pushes this to the limit: O(1) translation, ~11 ms compile for STS cocurrent heat exchangers from 10 to 81,920 segments, with arrays resizable after compilation.

On the Julia side, the SciML ThermalFluid benchmark (Carman et al.) is the most direct published MTK-vs-Modelica-tools scaling comparison: for a discretized district-heating pipe (4N states, N up to 1280), Dymola's translation stays near-flat (1.9 s at N=5 to 15.1 s at N=1280) while OpenModelica grows superlinearly (4.0 s to 329.9 s); ModelingToolkit/JuliaSimCompiler variants (Julia, C, LLVM targets) sit in between but pay tens of seconds of structural_simplify/JIT latency even at small N — the classic JIT-vs-AOT tradeoff (near-zero marginal codegen cost per run for C tools, high first-compile latency for Julia; MARCO shows LLVM-direct AOT can beat both). The OpenModelica.jl/MDPI 2022 study found the Julia-hosted compiler ~4x slower in compilation than C-based OMC on a transmission-line model with tens of thousands of equations, memory-limited to ~25,600 equations, though MTK-generated simulation code outperformed Dymola in one benchmark case. For building/district-heating models, Jorissen/Wetter/Helsen (Modelica 2015) documented the bottleneck taxonomy (algebraic loops, dense Jacobians, event chattering, integrator choice) and achieved ~500x real-time for a medium office building with envelope+HVAC+control after refactoring; the DEMO fusion-blanket model (289,126 eq., 20,772 states) ran a transient in 64 s (DD) vs 146 s (DA) in the 2017 study.

Sparse-solver adoption: OpenModelica integrated SuiteSparse KLU and UMFPACK (linear), KINSOL (nonlinear, sparse-capable) and IDA as sparse implicit integrator in 2016-2017, and its DAE mode (--daeMode) passes the whole index-1 DAE to IDA/KLU; tearing is deliberately disabled in sparse mode because tearing very large systems costs more backend time than it saves at runtime. Dymola exposes sparse handling via Advanced.SparseActivate and DAE-mode flags (documented in Dymola 2019 release notes); PARDISO (Intel MKL) is the standard HPC alternative but documented Modelica-tool adoption centers on KLU (circuit-simulation-derived, well matched to DAE sparsity). Notably, IDA/KLU-based DAE mode is now the recommended OpenModelica configuration for models above ~10,000 equations per the user's guide, and the ScalableTestGrids work sets it via vendor annotations.

Overall picture as of 2026: (a) scalarizing compilers show O(N)-to-O(N^2) translation growth with C compilation dominating; (b) array-preserving compilation (MARCO, OMC new backend, Modiator) reduces translation to near-O(1) in array size and is the only demonstrated route past ~1M equations (MARCO: 15M eq. in ~10 s); (c) sparse solvers are near-universally beneficial (2-60x) for large models; (d) DAE vs ODE mode has no universal winner except for dense-ODE/sparse-DAE structures like power grids where DAE mode is mandatory; (e) Julia/MTK matches or beats Modelica tools on runtime but pays significant JIT/symbolic-processing latency and memory at scale.
## key facts
- ScalableTestSuite (Casella): open library of Modelica models scalable via integer parameters, designed to test compile+simulate scaling; STS README notes the benchmark bottleneck 'is almost invariably C compilation', and comparing ScalableTestSuite vs ScalableTestSuite_noopt shows -O0 makes simulation 20-30% slower but C compilation 3-4x faster (github.com/casella/ScalableTestSuite README).
- Braun/Casella/Bachmann, Modelica 2017 (DOI 10.3384/ecp17132557), Table 1 (OpenModelica 1.12.0-dev, i7-6900K): sparse solvers (IDA+KLU+KINSOL) beat default dense DASSL+tearing in 7 of 9 STS benchmarks, speedups ~2x to ~60x. Example: TransmissionLineEquations_N_1280 (2,562 eq., 2,560 states) = 241.1 s dense-ODE vs 6.10 s sparse-ODE vs 4.97 s DAE-mode vs 4.62 s reduced-DAE.
- Same paper, DAE-vs-ODE tradeoffs: StringModelica_N_64 (20,575 eq.) 86.5 s OD vs 25.9 s OS vs 3.76 s DA (multibody favors DAE mode ~20x); TransmissionLineModelica_N_1280 (26,915 eq.) 9.01 s sparse-ODE vs 47.8 s full DAE (~5x penalty, redundant MSL algebraic variables); SimpleAdvection_N_12800 (25,602 eq.): 642 s OD vs 41.2 s OS vs 19.2 s DA; conclusion: 'the DA strategy never turns out to provide any substantial advantage over the second best choice', but sparse solvers are 'the recommended option' for large models.
- Power grid benchmarks (Modelica 2017, Xeon E5-2650): RETE_C Irish grid 56,386 equations simulates 20 s transient in 0.96 s (rtol 1e-4) / 3.18 s (1e-6); RETE_E 157,022 eq. in 8.80/15.22 s; RETE_G Italian high-fidelity grid 593,886 eq. in 59.95/123.19 s, all DAE-mode IDA/KLU. Grid models have sparse DAE but dense ODE Jacobian (phasor coupling of all generators), making daeMode structurally required.
- DEMO fusion reactor breeding-blanket cooling model: 289,126 equations, 20,772 states; transient simulated in 64 s (DD reduced-DAE strategy) vs 146 s (DA full DAE) in OpenModelica (Modelica 2017 paper, Sec. 3.3).
- Casella/Guironnet OpenModelica Workshop 2021 (ScalableTestGrids, OMC v1.17-dev): grid model with 2N^2 generators, 2N^2*M loads; 'code generation and compilation OK up to about 200 components', 'simulation performance good up to about 4000 components', usable 'with some patience up to 1000/2000 components'; further size increase 'requires breakthrough in vectorized code generation'. Tearing disabled deliberately: faster codegen, sparse solvers recover efficiency.
- Pop et al., 'A New OpenModelica Compiler High Performance Frontend', Modelica 2019 (DOI 10.3384/ecp19157689), Table 1 flattening times (HP ZBook i7-6820HQ): DistributionSystemLinear_N_112_M_112 (779,312 eq.): Dymola 2019 74.21 s, OMC new frontend 32.31 s, old frontend 1076.54 s (~33x speedup); TransmissionLineModelica_N_1280: 4.45 s NF vs 47.77 s CF vs 9.84 s Dymola; Vectorized SolarSystem n=100,000 (600,001 eq.): Dymola 14,458.7 s, NF scalarized 2,450.6 s, NF with -d=-nfScalarize 2.95 s (~5000x vs Dymola). Table 2: new frontend 18-200x faster than old on MultiBody getComponentAnnotations (FreeMotionScalarInit 28.90 s -> 0.14 s = 199x).
- OpenModelica old-vs-new backend (Abdelhak/Bachmann, OM Workshop 2022 'Pseudo-Array Causalization' slides): CascadedFirstOrder scaled to N=24,000 shows old backend total time approaching ~5,000 s and backend phase ~12 s vs new backend a small flat fraction of that (plots pp. 22-25); C compilation ~80 s at max N. Feb 2026 workshop slides ('Status of the New Backend') show array-handling still Work-in-Progress for tearing/algorithms modules.
- OMC v1.24 ScalableTestSuite regression report: 263 models, 259 pass frontend-through-templates, 243 simulate; multiple large instances hit limits, e.g. HarmonicOscillatorNetwork_N_320 300.02 s buildModel (timeout) with only 3.87 s simulation; DistributionSystemLinear_N_56_M_56 hits 925 s total timeout; CascadedFirstOrder_N_25600 builds in 18.78 s, simulates 118 s (libraries.openmodelica.org).
- MARCO (Agosta, Casella, et al., Modelica 2023, DOI 10.3384/ecp20413, Table 1, i9-12900KF/96GB): heat-exchanger network with 14,776,202 equations compiles in 10.13 s (simulation 33,574.76 s / 40,000 Euler steps); 3D thermal chip 4,465,160 equations compiles in 86.24 s (simulation 605.47 s / 60,000 steps). MLIR/LLVM-IR direct machine-code generation, no C step; authors 'not aware of any other Modelica tool' handling 15M-equation models, let alone with tens-of-seconds compile times. Limitations then: continuous-time only, no events, no index reduction.
- Fioravanti et al., 'Array-Aware Matching: Taming the Complexity of Large-Scale Simulation Models', ACM TOMS 2023 (DOI 10.1145/3611661, arXiv 2212.11135): proves optimal array-aware matching is NP-complete; proposes polynomial heuristic with MCIS/MCIM interval data structures giving effectively O(1) matching cost per array equation for PDE-discretization-style models.
- EOOLT 2019 'Towards a benchmark suite for high-performance Modelica compilers' (DOI 10.1145/3365984.3365988): OpenModelica tests of array-preserving compilation show build-time reduction of one-two orders of magnitude and 2-3x smaller generated code.
- Elmqvist/Otter 'Resizable Arrays in Object-Oriented Modeling', Modelica 2025 (DOI 10.3384/ecp218189): Modiator web-app compiles without expanding arrays; STS cocurrent heat exchanger compiles in ~11 ms independent of n from 10 to 81,920 segments; transmission line ~50 ms independent of n; O(1) compile time and arrays resizable after compilation (incl. array-level Pantelides index reduction without scalarization).
- SciML ThermalFluid benchmark (docs.sciml.ai/SciMLBenchmarksOutput): 470 m district-heating pipe, 4N states, N=5..1280 (20-5120 states). Translation/compile times: Dymola 1.92 s (N=5) to 15.09 s (N=1280), near-flat; OpenModelica 3.99 s to 329.92 s (superlinear, ~22x slower than Dymola at N=1280); MTK/JuliaSimCompiler (Julia/C/LLVM targets) intermediate, with structural simplification alone ~25-48 s at small N (JIT latency).
- OpenModelica.jl (Tinnerholm et al., MDPI Electronics 2022, DOI 10.3390/electronics11111772): Julia-hosted Modelica compiler targeting ModelingToolkit.jl is ~4x slower in compile time than C-based OMC on a transmission-line model with tens of thousands of equations; benchmarking capped at 25,600 equations by Julia/MTK memory requirements; MTK-generated code outperformed Dymola in one simulation benchmark case.
- Sparse solver adoption: OpenModelica added UMFPACK+KLU (linear), KINSOL (nonlinear), sparse IDA in 2016-17 (Braun); --daeMode passes whole index-1 DAE to IDA and only works with IDA; user's guide recommends sparse/daeMode for large models. Dymola exposes sparse activation via Advanced.SparseActivate and gained DAE-mode support around Dymola 2019 (Claytex release notes). KLU (circuit-simulation direct solver, Davis & Natarajan, ACM TOMS 2010) is the dominant choice; PARDISO/MKL appears in HPC literature but not as a documented default in mainstream Modelica tools.
- Building/district-heating bottlenecks: Jorissen, Wetter, Helsen (Modelica 2015, DOI 10.3384/ecp1511859) analyze algebraic loops, code efficiency, integrator choice as speed determinants and report a medium office building (envelope+HVAC+control, IDEAS library, Dymola) simulating ~500x faster than real time after model refactoring.
- MARCO motivation (RTE): French TSO RTE could not use Modelica tools end-to-end at national/continental grid scale and built the Dynawo simulation engine around Modelica component models instead (Masoom et al. 2021), cited by the MARCO paper as evidence of the ~1M-equation tooling threshold.
- JIT vs AOT tradeoff summary from the data: C-generating tools (OMC/Dymola) pay per-model C-compile cost that dominates at scale (STS README; OMC 330 s at 5120 states in ThermalFluid); Julia/MTK pays JIT+symbolic latency (tens of seconds even for tiny N, 4x slower compiler host performance, memory-bound at ~25k equations); MARCO's AOT LLVM-IR emission avoids both, compiling 15M equations in ~10 s while retaining compiled-code runtime speed.
